ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-11-10-DSPG128.u5	Mr. Alfonso Rueda Valenzuela spoke for the presentation of the project.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-11-10-DSPG128.u6	Good morning, Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen, today you present to the Galician Council in this House a law that meets a commitment expressed by the President of Galician Union in his January parliamentary presence this same year. At that meeting you will remember that the President of the Board has committed up to five legal texts with the objective of giving a democratic impetus in Galicia to the functioning of our institutions. Three of them are already approved today and the other two, including what is presented today, are already in this House for its processing. It has therefore shown that this commitment was serious, manifested itself with a willingness to do so, which is so much so as to say with a willingness to fulfil the objective that was expressed when it was expressed, that it was to regain the confidence of citizenship in public institutions. It is therefore not only a change of attitude, but it is the establishment of concrete legal measures that improve our democratic quality and therefore endorse that desire to change attitudes. I think it's important here to talk about the quality of democracy that we want. It's not a matter of looking at how it is done in some places to plebiscites that everything they want to legitimate and that everything is trying to hide it, it's not just a matter of saying that the Government that has the most votes is the Government, therefore, that it has all the power to do what it desires without more. In the end, the most democratic government, the Government that promotes a more quality democracy, is that government that decides within legality to establish more limitations. Government, therefore, is limited by law, limited by the institutions that have to exercise counter-power and primarily limited by the freedoms that all and every one of the members of the citizenship on which they govern, over which we rule. Therefore, from that concept of democracy – that is the concept of democratic quality that drives this law – we have developed this programme of democratic impulse that is based – as I say – on control and the very limitation of power. The Act of Judiciary Services of the Union, which has just entered Parliament, will extend the internal control of legality over the decisions of the Government. The reform of the Law of the Board of Auditors and the Law on the financing of approved parties expand the capacities of external control bodies on political activity. The law of citizen participation in Parliament will provide citizens with new methods of indirect control through the parliamentary initiatives it decides to exercise. And finally, this new Transparency Act will guarantee direct control mechanisms for the citizenship of public activity. This is therefore a bill designed for citizenship and not intended for the power of governments acting on citizenship. The Government imposes, above all, obligations, limitations and light on its actions; citizenship on the contrary; and as it could not be otherwise, it guarantees rights, information and a controlled government that decides to self-limit itself. That's why from the very first moment, we've wanted to build this bill with citizenship, asking for contributions to citizenship. Immediately afterwards, and as a result of the presence of the President of the Board, a forum of participation was enabled to receive these suggestions in character prior to the drafting of the preliminary draft. And from here I want to thank all those people and groups who have participated in this process for their collaboration. This was, of course, decisive, very good ideas to regulate without going further to the re-use and accessibility requirements of the information that is published, the characteristics of the independent control body or the relationship of high office to companies before and after their entry into government. All of these were contributions that he made to citizenship when asked for it. So, with these approaches and many more during these months, a preliminary draft law was drafted which was re-submitted to public exposure, which was forwarded to all entities affected by allegations and which finally arrived at this House based on four core action axes: that of active advertising, that of the right to access, that of the rules of incompatibilities and the regulation of the transitional period between governments. With regard to the first of the axes, that of the active advertising, to say that I believe that it is what we have been most advanced so far, thanks in large part to the law that had already existed since 2006, the law passed during the Bipartisan Government, which was unanimously passed in this Parliament and therefore welcomed by all political groups, and which already detailed at that time a number of obligations to publish information on the Internet, as early as 2006. But, no doubt, this bill, which now presents itself, is supposed to advance with respect to that model, for many reasons, has also passed almost ten years, and now the publication of this data extends to new subjects. For example, to all dependent entities in Galicia, before they were excluded in the law of 2006 and, no doubt, this was an important asset, it also extends to the statutory organs and also to universities, which were previously also excluded. In addition, information from the autonomic public sector will overcome the current dispersal, which makes it difficult to consult and access citizenship; it will be centralized on a single portal, which will also have guarantees of accessibility, updating and preferential dissemination in open and reusable formats. I insist: this is a direct contribution of citizenship in the consultation period. It's therefore about taking steps to increase information at the citizen's permanent disposal. It's been doing so a long time ago, as shown by the International Transparency, which is the body that evaluates this matter in all state administrations. And it has to be said that your score on the information you've been offering to the Galician Union has not stopped increasing in these years, as it has risen from 87 points in 2010, to 90 points in 2012 and to 94 over 100 in 2014. I mean, they're taking themselves over these years, as on the other hand was our obligation, fundamental steps and now is the time to take the big step forward with the adoption of this law. What are you going to do? For example, increase the transparency of our parallel administration. It's already increased in 2009, forcing dozens of people who used to make it not to integrate their budgets into the general budgets of the Board, we've already approved the salaries of the directors of all these entities, and we've not only audited all of the entities that had the Board of Galicia, but from now on they have to do it periodically, and it's not going to be a voluntary thing. We are now also legally obliged to have the salaries of the executive staff, their program contracts and their action plans published on the website. The citizenry, of course, has the right to know what these are doing and how much it costs. We will also increase the transparency of legislative activity. We have approved for the first time in the history of autonomy guidelines on regulatory techniques, which were very necessary and which will make our legal order more homogeneous, with similar processing criteria for all standards and therefore more understandable for citizenship. We are now going to force, also by law, to maintain access to all relevant legislation, to keep it accessible to citizenship, to be not dispersed and to be able to consult easily, as well as to publish texts of the judicial resolutions that affect the vigil of these norms and also those that affect their interpretation. And this, of course, especially for those citizens who develop their activity in the legal system, is a mechanism that will greatly facilitate access and information about our legislative body. There will also be greater transparency in public employment. A law has already been passed which restricts free appointment in intermediate positions, the Public Employment Act, and now legally we will also be obliged to publish in the Transparency Portal information on the personnel of the public at any time, information on the regulatory agreements of the labour committees and collective agreements, information on public job offers, on the personnel selection processes and on the number and cost of the union released. Transparency will also be increased in the Union's contractual activity. The Galician Contract Platform has already been launched. And the effective improvement and negotiation of the negotiated procedures has already been approved this year, and this new law will now have to publish also the composition of the contracting tables and the formalization of all contracts that formalise or sign the Galician Union. There will also be greater transparency in urbanism. Already the law of the soil being processed will force you to make the most of each step of the processing of a sorting instrument, but in addition to this Transparency Act it will be ensured that the Transparency Portal is at the disposal of the citizenry all general plans for municipal management, all its modifications and all its revisions updated in real time and in an easy way for you to consult. In total, therefore, more than 50 elements are included than have already been required by the Greek law of 2006 and by the basic legislation of the State which has been approved not long ago. With regard to the second axis, citizens' right of access, it must be thought that even though it is very wide, it is true – as I said – that the range of information that will be available on the network, the true qualitative leap in respect of previous legislation is the regulation of the right of access to public information. Ladies and gentlemen, this is not a law on the Galician board's website where the Administration is, the German Government is the only one who decides what hangs, what doesn't hang, but it's a law of rights where citizenship will decide which information you want to access, when you want access and how you want access to all the information. This right, which is true that it was first regulated in the State through the Basic Transparency Law, will be complemented, it will be reinforced in this bill, since it incorporates several of the recommendations that mark international transparency organisations. For example, it incorporates the right of citizenship to which public personnel orient and advises those administered for the exercise of this right of access to information, incorporates the allocation to a body independent of proven and contrasting, to the institution of the People's Valedor, the power to monitor compliance with this law and the role of resolving citizens' claims about information denied to it once it requests it. And it also incorporates a provision of sanctions for public officials who violate this right of access to the citizen. So by incorporating these precepts, the right of access will be substantially reinforced. In fact, if we adhere to the sixty indicators, which are also very accurate, that the Access Info organization uses to score the different transparency laws that exist throughout the world, this bill would even surpass the legislation of countries with quality of access to contrasted information, such as the United States, Canada or the Netherlands. This law would overcome them in those indicators, which – I say – are absolutely accurate and very easy to measure. It therefore maintains the essential foresight of the basic regulations I referred to earlier, that the request for information does not have to be motivated, and that the same limits are maintained for the right to access, which are none other than those set out in the Council of Europe Convention on Access to Public Documents, and which are also very limited limits, in order to promote the better possible access to the information requested. We seek in this law a change of paradigm or culture regarding the action of the Administration. Now it must be the Administration that is obliged to adapt, not the citizen to the Administration, but the Administration to the citizen, and that is why we also regulate the organizational changes, which will undoubtedly be needed within the autonomous public sector to respond effectively to new legal obligations. As a result, a fundamental right is regulated that until now the Welsh legislation did not recognize and that now it does, and reinforces itself with all those guarantee mechanisms that were not in the basic legislation. I mean, this law improves the basic law of transparency. This is the great qualitative leap to which I had previously referred: to consider that public information is the property of citizenship rather than of the Government, which simply custody and facilitates its consultation. Regarding the third axis, that of the incompatibilities, it has to be said that significant progress has also been made. The Incompatibilities Act of the Autonomous Community was on its way to 20 years of vigil, so it was clear that it was the right time for it to be updated on the occasion of the adoption of a Transparency Act. Of course, at a time when citizenship is justifiably showing its concern about the phenomenon, for example, of rotational doors, this bill takes decisive steps in this direction. The citizenry will know where they come from and what situation all the people who enter high office do. It will therefore be compulsory to include in the public declaration of activities performed in the preceding two years and business participations also in the years prior to the exercise as high office, and also to make public a capital declaration at the time of appointment of any high office. But also citizenship will know – this is an important novelty – what situation they are in and, above all, where those same people go. Therefore, a second capital statement will be required at the time of termination, in which one can see the property changes produced, if it were to occur, and also the communication to the Office of Incompatibilities of any destination after the termination. And it will be this autonomous body responsible for deciding whether or not there is a conflict of interest in that new destination and if it is not found and if it does not allow it to make public its decision not to authorize it and its reasons. But besides that internal-external control at the moments of entry and exit, also that extreme control of high office during your exercise. You are obliged to assume the high charge of abstention in any matter involving a company in which he or his spouse was bound in the two years prior to the taking of possession, you require the high charge of adherence to the ethical and constitutional code, which is further shielded by law, and there are sanctions, both of disability, i.e. sanctions in the administrative field, as well as sanctions in the economic system, for those people who violate the obligations of incompatibility and avoid conflicts of interest that are collected in the law. And finally, the last axis, which regulates the transcription between governments. To say that a government regulation is established in functions, what they can do, especially what they can't do and the mechanisms for the transfer of powers. This did not assess it by legislation, which was necessary to resolve and that it was even an electoral commitment to its resolution on the part of the party that supports the Government. So I clearly indicate, so that there is no doubt about it, since there was some doubt about the vigil of the law, that this government will be the first in the history of autonomy to see limited its actions in the period that touches it in function. So next year, when the House is dissolved, this government will not be able to compromise the following, be the Government whatever it is, either signing agreements, or convening public squares, or acquiring extraordinary spending commitments, thanks to the entry into force of this law. So it is not legislate for the next government, as one of the amendments to the whole, one of the arguments gathered in the amendments to the whole. Better said, only one point is made: the following government, – I insist – is the party it is, will also be the first in Galician history to receive a management report in which so important and so fundamental things are detailed to carry out future tasks, such as the economic situation that leaves the government out, the situation of the administration's staff, and all the obligations that it is subject to by the conventions or contracts that are in force at the time of the transfer of powers. With this law passed, therefore, opacity in the power transfer process will be impossible. So, ladies and gentlemen, and in short, these are the substantial advances that take place in these four axes where I tried to summarize everything that contains the law: expanded, accessible and reusable public data; right to enhanced and free access, right to access and information; hard and transparent incompatibilities; and clear guidelines for the transition between governments. I want to point out that every step I've taken is the first time that we've considered a legal text in the history of our autonomy. Therefore, our opinion, the view of the German Government, is that there are more than enough reasons to allow the parliamentary process of this law to be dealt with in Parliament, so that this law is debated in Parliament and, therefore, if it follows the spirit which has unanimously encouraged the adoption of the last law dealing with these issues nearly ten years ago. So we simply aspire to repeat that unanimity, we sincerely hope that the amendments that are made during the parliamentary processing period will enrich this text, which is undoubtedly likely to be enriched, and therefore we hope that the objective pursued by the collaboration and verification of this law, which is that citizenship trusts and fully trusts in its institutions, is achieved with the effort of all, with the contributions of all and with the final result that it satisfies everyone. Thank you very much.

That is, if you really want to come to a law of transparency in consensus, you can start by opening up to hear the opposition and accept its amendments. This bill is far from ensuring full transparency in government action, in addition to fleeing from using the law itself to encourage citizen participation, public interest in government action, linking this law with the rights and duties set out in the Citizenship Act. Thus, the principle of transparency itself is reduced to something as general as public activity, but there is no reference, for example, to transparency in public management. Among the guiding principles are several that I consider to be elementary, such as the principle of reciprocity in information with citizenship or the principle of linguistic normalization. Of course, the language normalization that you always forget. He does not seek the Government's obligation to inform citizens of the government's planning and its strategic commitments for each legislation, with the objective that citizenship can speak in relation to them and evaluate its social, economic and environmental impact, prior to its execution or legal amendment or enactment of the legislation necessary to bring forward the aforementioned Government's planning. Thus, no regulation or relationship of quality standards, good practices and corporate social responsibility that apply and on the evaluation of these standards appears in the bill; no quality indicators or quality control mechanisms are established through continuous evaluation, whose report is at the disposal of citizenship. In its scope, companies providing services to the Administration are not covered, but are, however, public money managers. Nor does it contemplate its application to the Church, at least as far as the management of the public money it manages. And public foundations are also excluded within their scope. In addition, as regards public procurement obligations, there are several levels of information which are not collected, such as the extensions granted in tenders or their cost for administration and their justification. In order to guarantee the right to information to all citizens, the right to send and receive all documentation in formats compatible with free software and in traditional paper format should be considered. When it comes to staff reporting obligations, try to disparaging trade union organizations with nominal distribution of time credit and the costs that originate. I do not oppose the publication of the number of libertees and institutional releases, their trade union distribution and total labour costs, but I find it indecent to make nominal distributions, because they are according to the professional category of each liberated or released union, and there are no differences in the remuneration of the rest of the workers and public workers of the same category. In any event, the information should also include the legal right to union representation, the importance of its work and the legality of the assistance of liberated and liberated trade unions. And despite the thorough information that is intended to be given in relation to the legal representation of workers and workers, the bill does not guarantee transparency in all matters relating to the recruitment of staff by the Administration, such as, for example, services commissions, subcontracted staff, implementing agreements for employees of companies with public participation or working for the Greek Public Administration. There is also a lack of information on eventuality rates in the Government of Greece, movement of recruitment lists, duration of temporary contracts, part-time recruitment and many other issues. As far as specific requirements for information on citizenship are concerned, there is a lack of information on the establishment of concerts with private companies for essential public services such as health, education, social services or transport, as well as public works adjudication, public-private partnerships and the privatization of public services. The obligation to provide information on the provision of public services and agreements forgets any information relating to compliance with standards of quality of services and employment in concessionary undertakings. Even so, the bill does not raise any obligations regarding the publication of health, structural and non-structural waiting lists, health waiting lists – for example, in the concerted centre of Povisa – the student who cannot access public education squares, the student who cannot access support services for students with special needs, the waiting list in social services, the waiting list in Risk, in day-care centres, in juvenile centres, in senior residences. That is, lack of information, and economic and budgetary information is reduced to minimum information on decisions already made, about which citizenship cannot influence, because it is not given instruments to do so. So it's worth transparency if that transparency isn't also associated with the possibility that citizenship will assess, control and can correct or propose alternatives to government action. The commitment to information on land and environment management could not be less, not even collecting the concept of the environment, by referring only to environmental information on water quality and greenhouse gas emissions. There's the question of whether you're talking about water, surface water, groundwater, continental water, marine water or all water. It would be essential that the environmental status of rivers and rivers in Greece — which is not included in the draft law either — should be made public, as would its degree of pollution, the main contaminant, the plans to fight against detected pollution, the investment that will be carried out in each one, the responsibilities that fall depending on each act of breach or deterioration of the environment and the distribution of the charges that will lead to its recovery. Consider the possibility that the reuse of documents published on the Transparency Portal will have commercial purposes, something which questions up to the Board of Auditors. It cannot be stated in a law that free public information, paid by all the Greeks and the Greeks, can be used for economic and commercial purposes. As for the rights of citizens of access to public information, the right to recast requests is not collected; the deadlines for answering the Administration are excessive; it does not include any body guaranteeing compliance with this law, granting the said responsibility to the valator or valuator of the people, who, however, are not given instruments to carry out this work; consideration is not established as persons with interest in requested information, nor the importance of motivating the request. In addition, all requests for information by the citizen should be made public that have been rejected and their motivation, because the bill is already responsible for limiting to the extreme the information to which they are entitled and not to citizenship, such as, for example, the High Charity Heritage Register, which will have a reserved character. Therefore, the high office will become known to one side and another will have reserved character. So much so that infringements do not include those relating to unjustified denial of information to citizenship, something that would be important, because if not citizenship is not protected when the information is denied. Therefore, that denial must be justified in delivering the information to the citizenry. In addition, the bill is very imprecise, and many of its articles, repeating verbs like "power", or sanctions that range from a minimum to a maximum, without specifying on what criteria those minimums and maximums are applied, which creates a genuine legal insecurity. The current Legislative Government for the following, since it excludes from the application of this law the formalized contracts, agreements signed or grants granted prior to the entry into force of this law. The bill also escapes from establishing the mechanisms necessary for denial of information required by citizenship to be an exception, which must always be duly justified. He simply gathered that one can answer or that one can deny the answer, but the denial of the answer to citizenship had to be something very exceptional, and so it should be stated in the law, and that this refusal had to be very justified so that the person to whom it was denied could claim and demand his answer on the basis of that justification. If we join this lack of commitment to establishing that administrative silence is understood as denial of information, it is clear that this bill will not respond to the social claim of transparency in public proceedings, and therefore we will be faced with a bill of clear electoral intent. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-11-10-DSPG128.u9	Thank you, Mrs. Martínez. For the Galician Nationalist Block Parliamentary Group, Mrs. Paz.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-11-10-DSPG128.u10	Thank you, President. Good morning, ladies and gentlemen, and Mr Vice-President of the Galician Union. As the vice-president said in his defense of this bill, it stems from that debate which took place in this Parliament at the end of January this year, with which Mr Núñez Feijóo intended to put an end to any political debate on corruption in our country; with which he intended to legitimately continue to hibernate the Commission on Prevention and Measures against Corruption that exists in this Parliament and which continues without having a dictatorship after many appearances have taken place in this Parliament, some - or many - in relation directly to transparency in the exercise of public office and public administration. Unfortunately, after almost more than ten months since that appearance of the President of the Council of Galicia took place, we can affirm almost with total roundness, and the content of this Law of transparency and good governance which today brings us to this whole debate, which practically all the measures formulated in that plenary end up being purely cosmetic. We can point out, for example, that, as a result of that debate, a reform of this House's Rules of Procedure has not even substantially improved the information mechanisms for Members and Members, and in which the People's Party has maintained its refusal to introduce any transparency measures in the law governing the functioning of this Parliament, or the participation of the citizenry, because it is supposed to come into the Transparency Act, and today we see that in the Transparency Act little comes to that, nor does it even lay down the obligation that the Register of Parliament, in those matters which simply have to do with the normal functioning of Parliament, be public, no longer only for citizenship, but it is not even public for the formally and legally accredited media. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen of the People's Party, among many other reasons, it is why since the Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block Galego we have formulated this amendment for return, because we believe that this bill which today begins its parliamentary journey essentially follows the doctrine or the path drawn up in the prevailing law in this very matter in the Spanish State, and that even though the two – which is already two years old – were born at a time of important demands, to improve the participation and control capacity by the citizenry of all public affairs, however, from our point of view, what is adopted is a model of transparency that is absolutely outdated, both in relation to the aspirations of most society and in which it does not take into account relevant models in other far more advanced states in the area of transparency. It is curious that even in this law, which is almost two years old since the entry into force of the state law, it has not even bothered anyone who has drawn up this bill to see the deficiencies that existed in the current applicable law to try to modify them through the approval of this law, and we are faced with a simple one, we could say – to speak quickly and plainly –, transfer of the content of that law to the autonomous legislative framework. Of course, we believe that through this bill there are no standards that can be approved with the rest of the consolidated democracies. It also matches the position of the Council of Europe. In recent years it has been producing a progressive reinforcement of the right of access to public information which had its turning point in 2009, coinciding with the recognition by the European Court of Human Rights as an integral part of a fundamental right, the freedom of expression and information, enshrined in Article 10 of the European Convention on Human Rights, in connection with Article 19 of the Universal Declaration of Human Rights. We are therefore faced with a right which, in the Spanish legislative framework, does not have the fundamental right, but neither does the Galician Union – and the People's Party through this bill, in the sphere of competence of the Galician Union – consider; it does not give the fundamental right to the right of citizenship to obtain all the information, to access to public information. It is not even recognized in the law – from our point of view – which is really the subject that has the right to access to public information, whether they are only natural or legal persons directly or collectively represented through associations or organizations. Therefore, we are before a law, from our point of view, very defective. We would like to answer a number of questions in this debate. In recent years we have seen important social scandals that scandalize citizenship in terms of how public resources are used, with how, from the point of view of different administrations, political decisions have been used, not only those directly related to how and in which their public resources are spent, but also with decisions in the legal field, in the regulatory field, which have scandalized citizenship. We have witnessed a growing process of privatization of public services, we have witnessed an ever-increasing increase in the amount of public resources that, by one way or another, are being managed or executed by companies or private entities and we have been watching, or are still attending, the important public scandals that have plagued citizenship and that come as a result of this derivation of public resources towards the private sphere, which is where it has been the biggest growing stock of all the corruption cases that we are experiencing today. And yet most of those areas that they manage, that they end up managing, a very relevant portion of the public resources of the budgets of the Galician Union, are not subject to compliance with that law. They are therefore beyond any possibility of criminalization on the part of the citizenry. I'd like to ask you a few questions in this regard: Does anyone believe that, if this bill were approved in terms of what is currently being drafted, for example, would prevent the future president of the Galician Union, when he is publicly aware of his connection with a drug dealer who hired the Government of Galicia, he could conceal from us documentation related to the Golden Marcinal Contracts? For I say yes, that with this law in force this might happen again. Or do you think that with this law in force, with this wording you have today, could you go back to Galician Council to hide contracts with companies in the Gürtel plot, as you have done to this Parliament, to these Members and to citizenship? This law should not have to take account of these contracts. Or ladies and gentlemen of the People's Party, the Vigo hospital is one of the largest infrastructure built in our country, an infrastructure that, if built directly by the Public Administration, would have cost about 400 million euros, which, through the public-private funding chosen by the Galician Union, will cost us all the Greeks and Greeks, about three times more. With this text of transparency and good governance that brings us here today the People's Party do you believe that this Parliament, or that the citizenship of Galician people, or that the groups of users affected by such a deficit hospital, can exercise some kind of control over these more than 1 billion euros that will go into the hands of the company that will manage and build Vigo's hospital? ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-11-10-DSPG128.u20	Mr. President. Counselor. Ladies and gentlemen, the Socialist Group has not tabled a complete amendment to this law. We understand that it brings the cause of Law 9/2013, of transparency, the State Law of transparency and access to public information and good government, which is a rule that established two years for its application by autonomous communities, which is exactly the next month. The Italian Executive, therefore, today presents, within almost the time limit set, a bill that replaces the rule passed by this Parliament in 2006 and which was then a pioneer initiative in Spain. The text presents remarkable innovations on current practice, also shows that we will try to correct the parliamentary process with partial amendments. But, as happened in 2006, we believe that the force of this law lies in a change of culture in the administration, also in the citizens themselves and in the powers of the Greek people, and that it seems to us that this cultural change must go beyond the differences on some aspects of the text and that it must give priority to a message of commitment with a necessary norm and demanded by society. In order to properly contextualize transparency as we are – said the counsellor in his intervention – it is sufficient to review the indices of the non-governmental organization International Transparency; a work that, as is done in the various institutions of the State, allows us to establish comparisons based on a set of indicators previously known by the evaluated entities themselves. Thus, among the autonomous communities, Galicia has been positioned, from the first position in 2010 to the fifth in 2014, just to acknowledge that as a result of the later integration of autonomous communities into the transparency process they acted with greater decision. If we talk about parliaments, it must be acknowledged that the Galician Parliament is not in a good position – I call the House Bureau's attention in that sense – a tenth position in the whole of the autonomous parliaments. As for the big cities, only three out of seven are above the Spanish average, and only two out of four Spanish deputies exceed that ranking. And if we look at the autonomous bodies analyzed, which are only the managers of hydraulic policies, we find that Galicia occupies the penultimate position. In short, much remains to be done in all areas of the Greek administrations. So, we are faced with a necessary standard, a standard that, as you know, is a prerequisite more than a solution. By itself transparency will not prevent corruption practices or make the Administration more virtuous, but without this rule the situation will be more prone to irregular power deviations and behaviors, as we unfortunately have been enduring. As the representative of the Government and as the spokesperson predictably, the spokesperson of the People's Group extends to the positive or novice aspects of the norm, allow me at this moment to make manifest shortcomings which this group detects or are you who seem to have to be corrected to have a law in line with the demands of today's society. Among the different issues that the law is insufficiently dealing with, in the opinion of this group, I want to stop at some that are at the root of the main causes of corruption in the Administration, according to generalized coincidence. For example, conflicts of interest between the public and the private sector. The rule only addresses this problem. I'll put three good examples documented. First example, health. The pressure capacity of the pharmaceutical industry, health technology companies or hospital resource management companies – hospitals, etc. – is being exercised through different routes. No doubt, as are large business groups, large pharmaceutical groups or service companies that have a great capacity for action, undoubtedly through the information agenda, strongly mediated by the views of these groups in almost inevitable ways. They also influence the training of professionals, through the mechanism of subsidisation of congresses, professional and scientific events, up to a level that has no parallel to any other profession in Spain. They also determine the research agenda, again through their funding capacity. And, on the other hand, there's no need to forget that they're ultimately acting on professionals who have enormous prescription power according to current regulations. By the way, on the action of pharmaceutical groups in hospital research there is minimal transparency at present in our country. And finally, there is political influence through the leadership capture mechanism, a situation that is repeated in countries around us. We have recent evidence of how that vested interest works at Vigo's new hospital. After several years of demanding it, the Board has not yet been able to offer solvent documentation that supports the chosen management formula. As an example, also in the field of health. It's documented as the vaccine conflict gave rise to a formidable business, with the convenance of some professional sectors. I remember some data. If in the United Kingdom 2% of minors were vaccinated against one of these diseases and 7% in France, doses were sold to 90% of minors in Spain. Public administrations took one year to react in a coordinated manner in the face of a clear bias in health policy openly promoted by commercial interests. There are published studies explaining how this mechanism has worked. A remarkable third example is the regulation of the electricity sector. The five major Spanish electricity companies, with benefits greater than their European counterparts, mark the Ministry of Industry's agenda on matters such as renewable energies or recently in the self-consumption decree, even when disregarding the reports of the National Commission on Markets and Competition. Thus, the Government adopts solutions other than those that are driven in the countries around us solely for the benefit of the five operators ahead. The transparency of the sector is minimal and the interests present in each problem are rarely explained. The conflict of interest also occurs at the level of staff members with the capacity to propose or to intervene by means of reports in the adjudication process. Nothing is spoken of in the project of this problem. Again the Vigo hospital has just offered a worrisome demonstration, where the follow-up of the contract was attributed to a person from the health administration and, therefore, with a deep knowledge of the mechanisms and actual limits of the control capacity of public services over administrative concessions. A recent report explains that 55% of state lawyers, that is, those who have the responsibility to defend public interest in the courts, have partial dedication, and 40% are in excess. It is not understandable that those who represent the general interest are captured by the private interest in conflict precisely with the State. It has happened, for example, in the Prestige accident, that we commemorate this week; however, the bill we are debating today does not go into this subject at all. Moreover, a bill has just been tabled in Parliament to regulate legal advice and grant a privileged status to the body of lawyers of the Union without the slightest reference to this problem we are discussing. It's logical that professionals corporately try to avoid excessive limitations to their professional career, but it's incomprehensible that Joine tolerates it and elevates it to the rank of law. We defend that transparency is extended to resolutions of compatibility authorisations which affect both public employees who have these optional reporting powers and senior positions after their termination. If you want to control an opaque practice that is being used as a favor payment, unconditional transvasion must be made difficult, which we colloquially call "rotate doors", setting limits both on exit and return. The project we are discussing also excludes from its application entities that this parliamentary group considers should be under its tutelage, for example, local administrations, commercial societies with public sector support above a certain threshold and even private entities receiving aid above a figure and in which such aid represents an important fraction of their income. We also detect problems in the treatment of public procurement. If we assume that the procurement has a multiplier effect on the economic fabric, we need to pay more attention to it and overcome the malfunctions that employers themselves report; for example, we propose to publish all the data on the assignment of contracts and subcontracts, a formula through which the Administration is allowing the public sector to deteriorate in competition, and we defend that the public procurement policy of the Administration is regularly evaluated as part of transparency. In addition, and by collecting the opinion of many experts also from Greece, we propose that all public investment projects greater than 5 million euros be subject to a prior cost-benefit analysis, which will improve their efficiency, and of course those assessments are public, whether they are direct execution projects or concessional projects or public-private collaboration projects. Again it's about preventing what happened to the new Vigo hospital or to the City of Culture. In the area of urbanism, we also propose extending public information to the resolutions and reports of the Higher Committee on Urbanism of the Urban Legality Protection Agency, because it is in urbanism where most of the cases of corruption have occurred. And we will propose that the concept of "legislative paste" be incorporated into all bills to know the reports and advice or opinions of entities other than the promoter body and that the interests concerned or involved may be valued by the public opinion. Finally, I wanted to defend the principle of quality regulation, the better regulation, which is absent in the legislative production of the Union, despite being driven and approved by the European Union, and this standard should be used to incorporate it. Because the glaring legislative production we are debating here in Parliament in recent years has a high degree of improvisation, as is shown by the continuous change in the standards adopted in recent times. This represents a level of legal insecurity and excessive regulation contrary precisely to the principles that drive the European Union, and also principles that are necessary because they directly result in the cost of companies' participation in the administration's procedures and even in essential procedures for economic life, such as the creation of companies, where, despite all the repeated promises, we continue to have some of the worst data in Community Europe in terms of time and cost. That legal insecurity is a topic when we talk about the Spanish administration and, of course, the Administration of the Union. For all these reasons, this group is not going to impede the consideration of the bill. We hope that the conciliatory will which the Counsellor has expressed today will not be hampered in the process by the opposing position of the majority group of reflections and amendments which I have just moved forward and which we believe would greatly improve the proposed text. Nothing else.

Full discussion of the draft law on tax and administrative measures
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-11-10-DSPG128.u44	Amendments to this bill were tabled by the Joint Parliamentary Group, the Galego Nationalist Block Parliamentary Group, the Left Gallaga Alternative Group and the Galician Socialist Group. For the presentation of the bill, the councillor of Fairy has the word for word.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-11-10-DSPG128.u45	Thank you very much, Mr. President. Good morning, gentlemen. I intervene in this plenary to present the Bill of Tax and Administrative Measures for 2016, a rule that complements the Bill of Budgets for the next exercise also currently in parliamentary processing. We seek to contribute to greater efficiency and efficiency in a set of measures related to different areas of activity with a vocation of permanence over time. It contributes to the achievement of certain multi-year guidance objectives pursued by the Community through budgetary execution. The doctrine of the Constitutional Court sets out that different measures, executives and regulations, and that by their nature they must adopt a rank of law, should not be incorporated into the annual laws of budgets but into specific laws as we present today. I would also like to remind you that the doctrinal debate on the nature of so-called accompaniment laws was resolved by the Supreme Court because the high court has set this type of rules as ordinary law whose contents are fully protected by the freedom of regulatory configuration enjoyed by the legislator. And that allows for better and more effective execution of the Government program in the different areas where its action is being developed. This bill includes measures to improve administration management and to increase its efficiency, to strengthen its simplification, to promote its rationalization and to bring legal security and protection to citizens. Thus this rule consists of two titles, one first, with measures of a tax nature, and one second, which incorporates others of an administrative nature. Within the tax section we include a package of actions to carry out the biggest tax reduction in the history of autonomy with the aim of increasing citizens' available income and encouraging consumption to make additional € 140 million available to the Greeks and the Greeks. As for the IRPF, we set a new scale applicable to the autonomic frame to improve its progress when passing from 5 to 7 sections. We have reduced and redistributed the tax burden by increasing the income available to families, promoting savings and investment and improving the competitiveness of our companies. So we've achieved a more equitable tax system by assuming a higher discount for middle and low incomes. With this downgrade we have completed this government two years ago, in force since the first of January 2014 when we advance the reform approved by the State and which has since benefited the liquidable bases of up to EUR 17,707, that is, 70% of the Greek taxpayers. This new discount is much more ambitious, as it will save up to 380 euros a year for low and medium incomes, which places Galicia as one of the lowest tax communities for these rents, and because it also benefits up to 98.5% of the Greek taxpayers. As far as succession and prayer tax is concerned, we have increased the reduction to the second group in general from the current EUR 18,000 to 400,000. With this measure, we intend to eliminate 99% of the cases of parental inheritance taxation, while maintaining an increased reduction for children aged 21 to 24 years between 900,000 and 600,000 euros. The goal is to keep the family heritage and the family's economic capacity to lose on many occasions the income that approached the deceased person. If the estate of the deceased is greater, we understand that the economic capacity of the family is sufficient to cope with the payment of the tax without it being significantly reduced; so we opt for the increase in personal reductions and not for the complete abolition of taxation, since Galicia already has a low type of recording for transmissions from parents to children of up to 18% against the most distant relatives. In addition, and given the existence of successor pacts in our Law 2/2006, of Galician civil law, as are the improvement and departure, it will allow to a greater degree than the present that those who want to deliver goods in life to their descendants, as an early form of inheritance to be perceived, if they benefit from this reduction. We also clarify in the Law of measures that the habitual housing reduction of the cause of inheritance tax will apply even if the person does not reside in it when, under physical or psychic circumstances, he or she moves to receive care to a specialized center or to live with his or her relatives. We thus comply with the text of a non-law proposal submitted by the People's Party and adopted unanimously by the rest of the groups last March; in this way we link the loss of living conditions habitually to the voluntary abandonment of it and not to the need to do so for medical reasons or to achieve their assistance and care. Galicia, as a whole, has one of the lowest taxes in all of Spain by the tax of successions and prayers; zero tax, as per direct heir to 400,000 euros. But we also consider measures within the property transmission tax and legal acts documented for the rural environment; in particular, we want tax not to be an obstacle to mobilising land with agrarian vocation when there is a demand for land by already-constituted agricultural holdings or for new initiatives. In addition, we have been complementing other existing ones since 2009 in support of the rural environment. A 100% reduction is set in the quota of rustic soil transmissions, which means that the recording is missing, so the tax is no longer a trap for carrying out these types of operations. We raise to 100% the reductions provided for in Law 19/1995, of modernisation of agricultural holdings, which means the tax benefit that will be complete for operations that seek to promote the transmission of agricultural holdings and the increase in their size. These percentages currently ranged from 50% to 90%, and we also set a 100% deduction on the record rate of documented legal acts that fall on the rustic prediary groupings, so we completed the zero-tax strategy in rural areas. Ladies and gentlemen, with the tax measures collected in this bill, we place the Greeks, along with the Madridians, as the taxpayers who will pay less taxes from all over Spain, and put at their disposal – as I said previously – 140 million euros. As far as taxation is concerned, in the area of administrative fees, we have amended Law 6/2003 for the creation of new rates or the modification of some of the vigors. Its economic impact highlights the rate for social services programmes and centres which sets three tranches, on the one hand, due to the different cost of accreditation check rates in the event of authorisation, responsible declaration and prior communication; and, on the other hand, the creation of a new rate necessary for the articulation of the verification to be carried out within the procedure of responsible declaration of commencement of the activity, supplementary service and substantial modification. With regard to water canon, it is clear what criteria should be used in the application of the water exemption by a social initiative entity, carried out in centres providing direct services to persons at risk or in situations of social exclusion. As far as administrative measures are concerned in this bill, they seek four questions: to improve administrative management, to increase efficiency, to deepen simplification and to provide legal security. With regard to management improvements in the field of social services, we have established the competences of the Union in the development of the protocols for the return of information between the different levels of action of the Greek social services system, establishing an express mention of the single electronic social history. We also seek to provide adequate coverage for the specialized care of children under 16 in residential centres for people with disabilities. In the wake of the housing we facilitate forced expropriation for social interest to the municipalities of those private or common elements necessary to perform works or facilities necessary to comply with the rules on accessibility and removal of architectural barriers. We guarantee that all the properties of credit institutions and their real estate subsidiaries and asset management entities will be included in the empty housing census of the autonomous community. Including banking restructuring procedures existing in buildings of collective residential type or in real estate complexes located in municipalities of more than 10,000 inhabitants. In the chapter on water, we declare of interest to the autonomous community the works included in the Greek Supply Plan and the Greek Sanitation Plan, as well as in the Hydrological Plan for the Galicia-Costa hydrographic demarcation. As regards the confrerery of fishermen, we have added a new precept concerning administrative intervention in government bodies, since the decree which develops the law of confrereries provides for this intervention but this provision is not collected in the law. In the field of research, we improve the model of innovation policy governance by integrating management into the follow-up of the Strategy of Intelligence Specialization RIS3, within the bodies provided for in Galicia's Promotion, Research and Innovation Act. In the context of economic and financial planning and management, we introduce the principles of economic and financial management, economic planning that will be ordered in strategic plans, sectoral and transversal plans and, finally, budgetary allocations to expenditure managers and the management of public expenditure, with the aim of focusing on the achievement of expected results. We intend to encourage compliance with the objectives of the management bodies of the Board, as a criterion more than one that is taken into account in the allocation of resources within the targets already set in the budget. In application of all these principles, the medium-term economic performance of the autonomous public sector will be ordered in the strategic plan of government, an instrument which sets priorities and gives coherence to the set of activities which the Union will carry out in the medium term. We will shortly present to this Parliament the 2015-2020 Strategic Plan, a living document in which, apart from bringing it to this budget to be improved in its case, we will implement budgeting the priorities of Galicia for this second half of the decade. In the area of the grant law, we coordinate grants on energy saving and efficiency, renewable energies established or managed by the entities referred to in the Law to avoid duplication in the concession and aid, in addition to technical advice to the management bodies in relation to the criteria of adjudication. We have also changed certain requirements in the official chambers and in the internal trade chambers to deepen the principle of administrative simplification, and we have eliminated the obligation to register in the Galego Trade Register as indispensable to opt for aid called by the Autonomic Administration or to participate actively in the specific programmes. Within the area of local administration, in order to promote advisory functions or for the voluntary incorporation of others, we have created a special fund with economic aid and technical criteria, as well as priority or special criteria in the allocation of finalist grants and in the organisation plans for autonomous services and the forgiveness of debts or increase of percentages in the local cooperation fund. We also in mobility simplify the procedures with regard to the Galician Transport of Tourism Vehicles Act to achieve greater rationalization of the administrative procedure. As far as energy is concerned, we protect consumers by setting by law the obligation to review invoices made since the installation of new smart counters and the return of amounts unduly charged, as well as the specific provision of an offender type to facilitate their sanction. We therefore have to bill on the basis of real consumption. In the field of public media, we guarantee, by responsibility, that the public radio and television service in Galicia will maintain an address until it is initiated by the Galician Radio and Television Corporation for its activity in the provision of public service. In the field of sport we also set accident insurance to provide the highest guarantees for the participants in sports events. In the field of tourism, we include the obligation of the Register of Active Tourism Companies to ensure the safety of activities benefiting businesses. We allow the establishment of more categories in tourist camps, we open ourselves to new specialties in the field of restoration with the inclusion of typical establishments in Galicia such as seafood or new types demanded as gastrobars. And we also added some supposed slight infringement in the specific case of tourist signage and on the Way of Santiago. In the field of agriculture, we have established the competent bodies to impose sanctions on infringements committed in the field of food quality. We have also modified the Law on Mountains with reports and land management tools and on urban planning, which must always contain reasoned technical assessments, with sectoral report from the Forest Administration. And, on the other hand, in the public employee's field, we recovered the days by antiquity through a union agreement; one more step in the recognition of the efforts of public employees during the crisis, as we are working in several areas both to improve the wage by anticipating the full return of the extra payment approved in 2012 and by law shielding the recovery, paid and retired, as well as for the recruitment of public professionals. All of them that have brought together a certain and progressive improvement in the working conditions of our workers – always within the budgetary availability to, as I say, advance in that recognition of the effort that the public employees have made during the crisis. Ladies and gentlemen, I am reminded that the measures included in this bill improve the day-to-day management of administration, promote simplification and rationalization, seek to improve efficiency and efficiency and bring security and protection to citizens. Thank you very much.

Thus, the doctrine asserts that these accompanying laws would be at the bottom a piece of the Budget Law that segregates, separates and deals parallel to the Budget Law, but avoiding the limitations these rules have. Debate and vote on the proposal for a framework mandate of the Corporation Radio and Television de Galicia S.A., adopted by Commission 1a, Institutional, General Administration, Justice and Interior
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-11-10-DSPG128.u94	By agreement of the Bureau of 10 November 2015 the procedure for the elaboration and approval of the framework mandate provided for in Article 32 of Law 9/2011 of 9 November 2011 of the public media for audiovisual communication in Galicia began. In accordance with the fourth rule of the regulators of this proceeding, the Commission 1st, Institutional, General Administration, Justice and Interior, adopted on 30 October 2015 the proposal for a framework mandate which is now put to the debate and vote in this plenary, together with the particular votes not accepted by the Joint Parliamentary Groups, the Nationalist Block Galego, the Alternative Galician Left and Socialists of Galicia, registration numbers 43083, 43103, 43105 and 43104, respectively. The debate will consist of a round of parliamentary groups for the defence of their particular votes, in reverse order to their numerical importance, followed by another round of the group that has no particular vote, and both for a maximum time of ten minutes. First, individual votes will be voted on, which will be adopted or rejected by a simple majority. The following shall be put to the vote: the proposal for a framework mandate adopted by Commission 1a, which shall be adopted by a majority of two thirds, i.e. 50 votes. We therefore begin with the turn of the parliamentary groups to defend their particular votes to the proposal for a framework mandate. We started with the Joint Parliamentary Group. You have the floor to Mrs. Martínez García.

ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-11-10-DSPG128.u144	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, the reason for this meeting is to give them an overview of the fundamental lines that will guide the work of the Social Policy Council. The goal is nothing other than to contribute to the well-being of the Greeks and the Greeks. After years of restrictions, arising from the serious economic crisis that has its origin in 2008, a new stage is now opened, with budgets increasing throughout the Union of Galicia, but particularly increasing for social policy. It's about taking this recovery from the public accounts to the Greek homes. We therefore want the 2016 budget to be closer to people and families with less resources, who most need the support of the Administration. That they serve to support more parents and mothers who decide to take the step of having a child; that they serve to care more and with better services the dependents and our older ones; that they serve to be with the people for whom recovery has not yet come and who have seen their quality of life diminish, because of the crisis, in recent years. In short, the priority that will continue to mark the performance of this board and of the government as a whole is people; a commitment to citizens and citizens who, as the administration does, are sealed with more budgetary resources. That's why the biggest budget margin we will have in 2016 is taken advantage of to give a new social impulse. From then on, the budget of the Social Policy Council goes up to more than 12 points, up to EUR 618 million; a budgetary reinforcement that will have a positive impact on each and every area of action in this autonomic department. So with regard to the previous exercise, support for children, family and conciliation will increase by almost 40%, up to EUR 97 million. Support for social inclusion will amount to 86 million euros, which means a 24% increase by 2016. Dependency support will reach EUR 324 million, i.e. EUR 26 million more than there was this year. Support for youth, participation and volunteering programmes will increase by 15%, reaching almost 10 million euros. We aspire, ladies and gentlemen, to make Galicia a better place to live and to form a family. We want to compensate for the efforts of many parents and mothers by giving them more facilities and support. We will be a sensitive administration, sensitive to the needs of families, and we will focus much of our efforts on supporting the development of the children of this country. This commitment must be reflected in the budgets, in the budgets of the family, children and conciliation area, which, as I said before, rise by 40% for next year, up to over EUR 97 million. In just six years, the Galician Union will have multiplied the economic allocation for families by two. We led this budgetary reinforcement to articulate new services, programs and aids, with which we intend to make Galicia a better place to have children, but above all a better place to be a child or to be a child. We refer to the most advanced countries with the greatest potential for the development of demographic policies, such as Finland, Sweden and Norway. Thus, based on experiences that are working in these countries, towards 2016 we will establish the following strategic lines. [[Murmurs.]] The good conciliation. We will extend this economic aid to parents and mothers who want to take their children to a child's school and don't have places in shelters right now with public funds. In the 2016 budgets there is an economic departure of EUR 3.4 million, which will allow the granting of 2,600 reconciliation bonds, which amounts to 1,400 new squares, which we will incorporate into all the squares currently financed by public funds. So it will be 1,400 new families of Greeks and Greeks who will have a public remedy for conciliation. [[Murmurs.]] The second program is the rural care bonus. We will enable an account of EUR 3 million to support families with children living in rural areas, who also have the right to have a reconciliation resource in their environment. We continue to move forward in the design of new reconciliation resources in personal, family and work life in rural Galician, another government strategic line. That's why it's going to give priority to municipalities where there's no offering of children's school squares. With good care in the countryside, we will also develop the children's homes, thus reaching a population of 0 to 12 years. With regard to the latter programme, four training courses are currently under way in the provinces of Lugo and Ourense. In addition to fostering reconciliation, and according to the line of the countries that previously reported, on the basis of the competences of the Galician Union, we will contribute to meeting the basic costs of the birth of a child by direct assistance of EUR 100 per month for the first year for each child and child born from 1 January 2016. This help will be to cover food costs, child hygiene, clothing and other necessities. For this purpose we have qualified in the budgets 9 million euros in 2016. And we want to continue this programme in the next few years, as you can verify in the order that we will approve this year and that it will come into force on January 1st, according to what was announced on your day in this House. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-11-10-DSPG128.u244	Thank you very much, Mr. President. And good afternoon to all and to all. In the last debate of the interpellation that takes place with this motion, the Counsellor concluded, in his speech, that closing schools in rural areas was a fact caused only by the failure of children and children in rural areas; a fact directly related and the consequence of the demographic crisis affecting our rural environment. He ignored, from our point of view, a central element, at least one of the central elements of this debate, which is not another, precisely, that the role that school has as one of the fundamental actors in the vertebration of a people, in its own dynamization, in addition. The counsellor focused on his intervention – as he usually does – to make the Government of Greece a single dimension of the problem: the purely economical dimension and profitability; forgetting, from our point of view, variables of much greater importance. The counsellor – and he deliberately did – has forgotten another fundamental dimension of rural school as an essential asset of the institutional system of a particular territory, of a certain people. I want to mention at this point the definition that Prats clearly made in this regard in 2004, and he says textually: "The school is an element that is part of the institutional structure within a given territory...;" - in our case the rural one - "...is a more actor among the different actors that has the effectiveness and equity of the social group in which it is integrated. The disappearance of the school means the disappearance of the feeling of the people habitable and also of the rupture of the institutional system itself, and, consequently, of the organizational and identity dimension of the rural environment." I think Prats clearly explains what I tried to explain to Mr. Counsellor when he said that, when a school is closed, when a school is closed, if a final death sentence is signed for a people, for a village. There's a second dimension, Counselor, that this government also forgets often; I'm talking about school as a receiver of identities and emotions from a particular community, of a certain people. The school – and in addition to the school especially in the rural environment – is an institution where the whole community feels recognized and identified. In the towns and villages there is a feeling of belonging to the school. Families feel that their children and their daughters belong to the school, but they also feel that the school belongs to the whole community. It is therefore an absolutely reciprocal feeling of belonging. So, at school dance and build experiences, emotions and experiences of a collective character that are essential for the vertebration of a people, for the vertebration of a community. When you close a school, you pull out, you delete a living element, not just a service, but a living and essential element for a particular community. And so we reach a third dimension, which, from our point of view, is fundamental to justifying the defence of public education in rural areas. And it's the dimension of school as a configuration element of the social construction of rural life. I refer to the role of school as a link between traditions and innovation, culture and society, ethics and values of a people and a community, and so on, a long and so forth. In short, these three dimensions – and not just the economist, which is the one employed by the Government of Greece – make communities at no time hesitate to contribute with their time, with their money and with their efforts to defend and empower the school of their people. We have many examples of these situations. We have examples of families working together in the school arrangements of their neighborhood, their people. We have examples of families who don't hesitate to help when a particular material needs to be re-assembled or when any pedagogical activity has to be organized. The families, the educational community, feel the school like something alive and something yours. And it's precisely because of that triple dimension based on that trust and reciprocity between the school and the people, between the neighbors and the education system, so families defend the school of their villages and their people, and that's why they mobilize when there's an announcement of closing a school, especially in rural areas. It is because of this triple dimension, institutional, social and emotional and identity, so the rural education system is fundamental to the survival not only of education and school in rural areas, but of Galicia as a people, for the reasons I have just said. And these are the reasons why they ultimately justify the need to give a serious and determined boost to support the education system in rural areas. Today we hear the new Social Policy Advisor talk about a serious demographic problem in our country, and we announce measures that are vague notices in the form of patches to solve this situation. Well, I said it in the interpellation of the full past and I repeat it now. There's no solution to Galicia's demographic problem that doesn't happen to boost school in rural areas, the education system in rural areas, there isn't. And, regretting that, Counselor, there is no such support. There is no support, nor is that support less determined by the Government of Greece. You, Counselor, clearly said it in the full past: schools are broken because there are no children. And I say something that is totally contrary to what you say: there are no children because they close schools, there are no children because they close health centers, there are no children because they close farms and livestock farms, there are no children because they close doors in the countryside, doors that don't open again. We would have to ask ourselves: if the Government's intention is really to help, empower and encourage schools in rural areas, why do not preventive measures be activated before the final closure of a school? For the truth is that the Government remains closed by arms, crossed by arms, waiting for a school to reach that fateful number of five students; and when that minimum number is not met, it definitely closes. That's why we bring this initiative, because we believe that the Government's obligation is to anticipate at that moment, to that fateful point, and because we believe that you have an obligation to do preventive work in a matter which is fundamental, as I said at first, for the future of Galicia. Today, at the time of tabling the motion, this is the time when we have to make the proposals. The complaint has already been made, Counselor. Since his reign Mr. Feijóo has closed 500 classes in Galicia in rural areas, more than 100 schools have disappeared forever. They are now going to make the proposals to us, and they are very concrete and understandable. In no case will he serve the economic argument. The proposals we make can simply be made with political will. First of all, we believe that the constitution, which Parliament constitutes a parliamentary commission on the situation of education in rural areas, is essential, as it was said, with the participation of social agents, the associations of parents and mothers of students and the municipalities concerned to analyse exactly - it may be that AGE is not right, of course - what is the strict situation of our teaching in rural areas. Secondly, we propose in this motion to create an observatory, the Galego Observatory of Teaching in Rural, to follow up the specific and specific needs of rural education; to propose specific training actions for rural and teacher education; to create a documentary and material production centre for promoting research and innovation within rural education. Thirdly, we propose the establishment of a monitoring and control committee on the evolution of the enrolment of rural schools in danger of closure. We want the Government to act on measures before these centres close, and when the registration decreases and approaches that number, as I said, announcing the imminent closure, preventive measures are activated; measures to activate mechanisms for that centre to survive in the future. And, fourthly and finally, we propose to create the figure of the Rural Centres for Educational Research and Innovation, the CRIEs, which are covered by state legislation, existing in other communities, and which are fundamental centers for the dynamization of rural education, promoting the collaboration of the different communities and educational communities, as centres of documentation, as centres of innovation and, above all, to put into value the huge educational capital that brings together our rural education in Galicia. These are measures that the Government can take and we hope that in this case the majority group will take, if it is willing to defend education in the rural areas of Galicia. Nothing else for now.
